Weak universality for a class of
3d stochastic reaction–diffusion models

M. Furlan

CEREMADE

Université Paris Dauphine, France

Email: furlan@ceremade.dauphine.fr

M. Gubinelli

IAM & HCM

Universität Bonn, Germany

Email:

gubinelli@iam.uni-bonn.de

Abstract

We establish the large scale convergence of a class of stochastic weakly nonlinear reaction–diffusion models on a three dimensional periodic domain to the dynamic \(\Phi^4_3\) model within the framework of paracontrolled distributions. Our work extends previous results of Hairer and Xu to nonlinearities with a finite amount of smoothness (in particular \(C^9\) is enough). We use the Malliavin calculus to perform a partial chaos expansion of the stochastic terms and control their \(L^p\) norms in terms of the graphs of the standard \(\Phi^4_3\) stochastic terms.

Keywords: weak universality, paracontrolled distributions, stochastic quantisation equation, Malliavin calculus, partial chaos expansion.

1Introduction

Consider a family of stochastic reaction–diffusion equation in a weakly nonlinear regime:

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} u (t, x) = - \varepsilon^{\alpha} F_{\varepsilon} (u (t, x)) + \eta (t, x), \qquad (t, x) \in [0, T / \varepsilon^2] \times (\mathbb{T}/ \varepsilon)^3\) (1)

with \(\varepsilon \in (0, 1]\), \(T > 0\), initial condition \(\bar{u}_{0, \varepsilon} : (\mathbb{T}/ \varepsilon)^3 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}\)\(\), \(F_{\varepsilon} \in C^9 (\mathbb{R})\) with exponential growth at infinity, \(\alpha > 0\) and \(\mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} : = (\partial_t - \Delta)\) the heat flow operator and \(\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/ (2 \pi \mathbb{Z})\). Here \(\eta\) denotes a family of centered Gaussian noises on \([0, T / \varepsilon^2] \times (\mathbb{T}/ \varepsilon)^3\) with stationary covariance

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} (\eta (t, x) \eta (s, y)) = \tilde{C}^{\varepsilon} (t - s, x, y)\)

such that \(\)\(\tilde{C}^{\varepsilon} (t - s, x, y) = \Sigma (t - s, x - y)\) if \(\operatorname{dist} (x, y) \leqslant 1\) and \(0\) otherwise where \(\Sigma : \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}^3 \rightarrow \mathbb{R}^+\) is a smooth, positive function compactly supported in \([0, 1] \times B_{\mathbb{R}^3} (0, 1)\). We assume also that there exists a compactly supported function \(\psi\) such that \(\psi \ast \psi = \Sigma\) (this is true e.g. when \(\eta\) is obtained by space-time convolution of the white noise with \(\psi\)).

We look for a large scale description of the solution to eq. (1) and we introduce the “mesoscopic” scale variable \(u_{\varepsilon} (t, x) = \varepsilon^{- \beta} u (t / \varepsilon^2, x / \varepsilon)\) where \(\beta > 0\). Substituting \(u_{\varepsilon}\) into (1) we get

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} u_{\varepsilon} (t, x) = - \varepsilon^{\alpha - 2 - \beta} F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\beta} u_{\varepsilon} (t, x)) + \varepsilon^{- 2 - \beta} \eta \left( \frac{t}{\varepsilon^2}, \frac{x}{\varepsilon} \right) . \) (2)

In order for the term \(\varepsilon^{- 2 - \beta} \eta (t / \varepsilon^2, x / \varepsilon)\) to converge to a non–trivial random limit we need that \(\beta = 1 / 2\). Indeed the Gaussian field \(\eta_{\varepsilon} (t, x) : = \varepsilon^{- 5 / 2} \eta (t / \varepsilon^2, x / \varepsilon)\) has covariance \(\varepsilon^{- 5} \tilde{C}^{\varepsilon} (t / \varepsilon^2, x / \varepsilon)\) and converges in distribution to the space-time white noise on \(\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3\). For large values of \(\alpha\) the non–linearity will be negligible with respect to the additive noise term. Heuristically, we can attempt an expansion of the reaction term around the stationary solution \(Y_{\varepsilon}\) to the linear equation

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} Y_{\varepsilon} = - Y_{\varepsilon} + \eta_{\varepsilon}, \) (3)

i.e. \(Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x) = \int_{- \infty}^t \check{P} (t - s, x - y) \eta_{\varepsilon} (s, y) \mathrm{d} s \mathrm{d} y\) with \(\check{P} (t, x) = \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{3 / 2}} e^{- \frac{| x |^2}{4 t}} e^{- t} \mathbb{1}_{t \geqslant 0}\).

Let us denote with \(C_{\varepsilon}\) the covariance of \(Y_{\varepsilon}\). We approximate the reaction term as

\(\displaystyle \varepsilon^{\alpha - 5 / 2} F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} u_{\varepsilon} (t, x)) \simeq \varepsilon^{\alpha - 5 / 2} F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)) .\)

The Gaussian r.v. \(\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)\) has variance \(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^2 = \varepsilon \mathbb{E} [ (Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x))^2] = \varepsilon \mathbb{E} [ (Y_{\varepsilon} (0, 0))^2] = \varepsilon C_{\varepsilon} (0, 0)\) independent of \((t, x)\). Although \(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^2\) depends on \(\varepsilon\)\(\), it can be bounded from above and below by two positive constants uniformly on \(\varepsilon \in (0, 1]\). We can expand the random variable \(F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x))\) according to the chaos decomposition relative to \(\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)\) and obtain

\(\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)) = \sum_{n \geqslant 0} f_{n, \varepsilon} H_n (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x), \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2), \) (4)

where \(H_n (x, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2)\) are standard Hermite polynomials with variance \(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^2\) and highest-order term normalized to \(1\). Note also that the coefficients \((f_{n, \varepsilon})_{n \geqslant 0}\) do not depend on \((t, x)\) by stationarity of the law of \(\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)\) since they are given by the formula

\(\displaystyle f_{n, \varepsilon} = \frac{1}{n! \sigma^{2 n}_{\varepsilon}} \mathbb{E} [F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)) H_n (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x), \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2)] = n!\mathbb{E} [F_{\varepsilon} (\sigma_{\varepsilon} G) H_n (\sigma_{\varepsilon} G, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2)]\)

where \(G\) is a standard Gaussian variable of unit variance.

Let \(X\) be the stationary solution to the equation

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} X = - X + \xi,\)

with \(\xi\) the space–time white noise on \(\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3\) and denote by \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^N \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) the generalized random fields given by the \(N\)-th Wick power of \(X\), which are well defined as random elements of \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3)\) as long as \(N \leqslant 4\). Denote with \(C_X\) the covariance of \(X\). The Gaussian analysis which we set up in this paper shows in particular that if \(\varepsilon^{(n - N) / 2} f_{n, \varepsilon} \rightarrow g_n\) as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) for every \(0 \leqslant n \leqslant N\), \(N \leqslant 4\), and \((F_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon} \subseteq C^{N + 1} (\mathbb{R})\) with exponential growth, then the family of random fields

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{F}^N_{\varepsilon} : (t, x) \mapsto \varepsilon^{- N / 2} F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)), \qquad (t, x) \in \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3,\)

converges in law in \(\mathcal{S}' (\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3)\) as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) to \(\sum_{n = 0}^N g_n \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^n \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\).

Consider the smallest \(n\) such that \(f_{n, \varepsilon}\) converges to a finite limit as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\). Since \(H_n (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) = \varepsilon^{n / 2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^n \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), the \(n\)-th term in the expansion (4) is \(f_{n, \varepsilon} \varepsilon^{\alpha + (n - 5) / 2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^n \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\). Therefore, the equation yields a non-trivial limit only if \(\alpha = (5 - n) / 2\). We are interested mainly in the case \(n = 3 \Rightarrow \alpha = 1\) and \(n = 1 \Rightarrow \alpha = 2\). The case \(\alpha = 2\) gives rise to a Gaussian limit and its analysis its not very difficult. In the following we will concentrate in the analysis of the \(\alpha = 1\) case where the limiting behaviour of the model is the most interesting and given by the \(\Phi^4_3\) family of singular SPDEs. In this case we obtain the family of models

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} u_{\varepsilon} (t, x) = - \varepsilon^{- \frac{3}{2}} F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} u_{\varepsilon} (t, x)) + \eta_{\varepsilon} (t, x) \quad \) (5)

with initial condition \(u_{0, \varepsilon} (\cdot) := \varepsilon^{- \frac{1}{2}} \bar{u}_{0, \varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{- 1} \cdot)\) where \(\bar{u}_{0, \varepsilon}\) is the initial condition of the microscopic model (1).

In order to state our main result, Theorem 2 below, let us introduce some notations and specify our assumptions. Let \(\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}\) be the centering (up to the third Wiener chaos relative to \(\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)\)) of the function \(F_{\varepsilon}\), i.e.

\(\displaystyle \tilde{F}_{\varepsilon} (x) := F_{\varepsilon} (x) - f_{0, \varepsilon} - f_{1, \varepsilon} x - f_{2, \varepsilon} H_2 (x, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) = \sum_{n \geqslant 3} f_{n, \varepsilon} H_n (x, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) . \) (6)

The decomposition of \(\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}\) is obviously the same as in (4) except for the fact that we have discarded the orders 0,1,2. \(\)Let \(\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{ (m)}\) be the \(m\)-th derivative of the function \(\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}\) for \(0 \leqslant m \leqslant 9\) and define the following \(\varepsilon\)–dependent constants:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} & := & \frac{\varepsilon^{- 2}}{9} \int_{s, x} P_s (x) \mathbb{E} [\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{ (1)} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (s, x)) \tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{ (1)} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (0, 0))],\\ \bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} & := & 2 \varepsilon^{- 1 / 2} {f_{3, \varepsilon}} f_{2, \varepsilon} \int_{s, x} P_s (x) (C_{\varepsilon} (s, x))^2,\\ d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} & := & \frac{\varepsilon^{- 2}}{6} \int_{s, x} P_s (x) \mathbb{E} [\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{ (0)} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (s, x)) \tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{ (2)} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (0, 0))],\\ d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} & := & \frac{\varepsilon^{- 5 / 2}}{3} \int_{s, x} P_s (x) \mathbb{E} [\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{ (0)} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (s, x)) \tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{ (1)} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} (0, 0))], \end{array} \) (7)

where \(P_s (x)\) is the heat kernel and \(\int_{s, x}\) denotes integration on \(\mathbb{R}^+ \times \mathbb{T}^3\).

Assumption 1. All along the paper we enforce the following assumptions:

  1. \((u_{0, \varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\) converges in law to a limit \(u_0\) in \(\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}\) and is independent of \(\eta\);

  2. \((\bar{u}_{0, \varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\) is uniformly bounded in \(L^{\infty}\) in probability, i.e. \(\exists C > 0\) such that \(\forall \varepsilon \in (0, 1]\) \(\| \bar{u}_{0, \varepsilon} \|_{L^{\infty} ((\mathbb{T}/ \varepsilon)^3)} \leqslant C\);

  3. \((F_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon} \subseteq C^9 (\mathbb{R})\) and there exists constants \(c, C > 0\) such that

    \(\displaystyle \sup_{\varepsilon, x} \sum_{k = 0}^9 | \partial_x^k F_{\varepsilon} (x) | \leqslant C e^{c | x |}, \) (8)

  4. the family of vectors \(\lambda_{\varepsilon} = (\lambda_{0, \varepsilon}, \lambda_{1, \varepsilon}, \lambda_{2, \varepsilon}, \lambda_{3, \varepsilon}) \in \mathbb{R}^4\) given by

    \(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lllllll} \lambda_{3, \varepsilon} & := & f_{3, \varepsilon} & & \lambda_{1, \varepsilon} & := & \varepsilon^{- 1} f_{1, \varepsilon} - 9 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} - 6 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}}\\ \lambda_{2, \varepsilon} & := & \varepsilon^{- 1 / 2} f_{2, \varepsilon} & & \lambda_{0, \varepsilon} & := & \varepsilon^{- 3 / 2} f_{0, \varepsilon} - \varepsilon^{- 1 / 2} f_{2, \varepsilon} d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} - 3 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} - 3 \bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} \end{array} \) (9)

    has a finite limit \(\lambda = (\lambda_0, \lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) \in \mathbb{R}^4\) as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\).

Theorem 2. (Convergence of the solution) Under Assumption 1 the family of random fields \((u_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\) given by the solution to eq. (5) converges in law and locally in time to a limiting random field \(u (\lambda)\) in the space \(C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} (\mathbb{T}^3)\) for every \(1 / 2 < \alpha < 2 / 3\). The law of \(u (\lambda)\) depends only on the value of \(\lambda\) and not on the other details of the nonlinearity or on the covariance of the noise term. We call this limit the dynamic \(\Phi^4_3\) model with parameter vector \(\lambda \in \mathbb{R}^4\).

Here \(C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} (\mathbb{T}^3)\) denotes the space of continuous functions from \([0, T]\) to the Besov space \(\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} (\mathbb{T}^3) = B^{- \alpha}_{\infty, \infty} (\mathbb{T}^3)\) (see Appendix A.1 for the notation on Besov spaces and paraproducts). Theorem 2 is actually just a corollary of the more precise result Theorem 7, in which we identify the paracontrolled equation satisfied by the limit random field \(u (\lambda)\).

Remark 3. We are interested only in local-in-time convergence of \(u_{\varepsilon}\), as a way to show the potential of our method for controlling stochastic terms with infinite chaos decomposition (developed in Section 26). Nevertheless, we expect it to be possible to obtain global-in-time convergence of the solution with more stringent assumptions on \(F_{\varepsilon}\), although we do not treat this problem here.

Remark 4. As a special case we can take

\begin{eqnarray*} F_{\varepsilon} (x) & = & \lambda_3 H_3 (x, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) + \varepsilon^{1 / 2} \lambda_2 H_2 (x, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) + \varepsilon (\lambda_1 + \gamma_{1, \varepsilon}) H_1 (x, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) + \varepsilon^{3 / 2} (\lambda_0 + \gamma_{0, \varepsilon}) \end{eqnarray*}

so that

\(\displaystyle f_{3, \varepsilon} = \lambda_3, \quad \varepsilon^{- 1 / 2} f_{2, \varepsilon} = \lambda_2, \quad \varepsilon^{- 1} f_{1, \varepsilon} = \lambda_1 + \gamma_{1, \varepsilon}, \quad \varepsilon^{- 3 / 2} f_{0, \varepsilon} = \lambda_0 + \gamma_{0, \varepsilon},\)

and

\(\displaystyle d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} = (\lambda_3)^2 L_{\varepsilon}, \quad \bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} = \lambda_3 \lambda_2 L_{\varepsilon}, \quad d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} = d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} = 0,\)

where \(L_{\varepsilon} := 2 \int_{s, x} P_s (x) (C_{Y, \varepsilon} (s, x))^2 .\) Choosing

\(\displaystyle \gamma_{1, \varepsilon} := 9 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} = 9 (\lambda_3)^2 L_{\varepsilon}, \qquad \gamma_{0, \varepsilon} := 3 \bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} = 3 \lambda_3 \lambda_2 L_{\varepsilon},\)

we obtain \(\lambda_{\varepsilon} \rightarrow (\lambda_0, \lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3)\). This shows that all the possible limits \(\lambda \in \mathbb{R}^4\) are attainable. In this case (5) takes the form

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} u_{\varepsilon} = - \lambda_3 u_{\varepsilon}^3 - \lambda_2 u_{\varepsilon}^2 - [\lambda_1 - 3 \lambda_3 \varepsilon^{- 1} \sigma^2_{\varepsilon} + 9 (\lambda_3)^2 L_{\varepsilon}] u_{\varepsilon} - \lambda_0 + \lambda_2 \sigma^2_{\varepsilon} - 3 \lambda_3 \lambda_2 L_{\varepsilon} + \eta_{\varepsilon} . \) (10)

The name dynamic \(\Phi^4_3\) equation (or stochastic quantisation equation) derives from the fact that the simplest class of models which approximate the limiting random field \(u (\lambda)\) is precisely obtained by choosing a cubic polynomial like in (10) as non-linear term (which is the gradient of a fourth order polinomial playing the role of local potential).

In two dimensions, this model has been subject of various studies since more than thirty years [12, 1, 5]. For the three dimensional case, the kind of convergence results described above are originally due to Hairer [8, 9] and constitute one of the first groundbreaking applications of his theory of regularity structures. Similar results were later obtained by Catellier and Chouk [4] using the paracontrolled approach of Gubinelli, Imkeller and Perkowski [6]. Kupiainen [13] described a third approach using renormalization group ideas.

Weak universality is the observation that the same limiting object describes the large scale behaviour of the solutions of more general equations, in particular that of the many parameters present in a general model, only a finite number of their combinations survive in the limit to describe the limiting object. The adjective “weak” is related to the fact that in order to control the large scale limit the non-linearity has to be very small in the microscopic scale. This sets up a perturbative regime which is well suited to the analysis via regularity structures or paracontrolled distributions.

The first result of weak universality for a singular stochastic PDE has been given by Hairer and Quastel [10] in the context the Kardar–Parisi–Zhang equation. Using the machinery developed there Hairer and Wu [11] proved a weak universality result for three dimensional reaction–diffusion equations in the case of Gaussian noise and a polynomial non–linearity, within the context of regularity structures. Weak universality for reaction–diffusion equations driven by non Gaussian noise is analysed in Shen and Wu [22]. Recently, important results concerning the stochastic quantisation equation we obtained by Mourrat and Weber. In particular the convergence to the dynamic \(\Phi^4_2\) model for a class of Markovian dynamics of discrete spin systems [15] and also the global wellposedness of \(\Phi^4_2\) in space and time [16] and in time for \(\Phi^4_3\) [17]. The recent preprint [21] analyzes an hyperbolic version of the stochastic quantisation equation in two dimensions, including the associated universality in the small noise regime.

The present work is the first which considers in detail the weak universality problem in the context of paracontrolled distributions, showing that on the analytic side the apriori estimates can be obtained via standard arguments and that the major difficulty is related to showing the convergence of a finite number of random fields to universal limiting objects. The main novelty of our work is our use of the Malliavin calculus [20, 19] to perform the analysis of these stochastic terms without requiring polynomial non–linearity as in the previous works cited above. In particular we were inspired by the computations in [25] and in general by the use of the Malliavin calculus to establish normal approximations [19]. The main technical results of our paper, Theorem 8 below, is not particularly linked to paracontrolled distributions. A similar analysis is conceivable for the stochastic models in regularity structures. Moreover the same tools can also allow to prove similar non-polynomial weak universality statements for the KPZ along the lines of the present analysis. This is the subject of ongoing work.

Acknowledgments 5. The authors would like to thank the anonymous referee for the detailed and constructive critique which contributed to improve the overall exposition of the results. Support via SFB CRC 1060 is also gratefully acknowledged.

2Analysis of the mesoscopic model

The goal of this section is to obtain a paracontrolled structure for equation (5) analogous to that introduced in [4] for the cubic polynomial case and use it to set up the limiting procedure. Convergence of the stochastic terms and some apriori estimates will be the subject of the following sections. Definitions and a reminder of the basic results of paradifferential calculus needed here can be found in Appendix A.

2.1Paracontrolled structure

Let us start our analysis by centering the reaction term \(F_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} u_{\varepsilon})\) in (5) using decomposition (6) to obtain:

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} u_{\varepsilon} & = & - \varepsilon^{- \frac{3}{2}} \tilde{F}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} u_{\varepsilon} (t, x)) + \eta_{\varepsilon}\\ & & - \varepsilon^{- 3 / 2} f_{0, \varepsilon} - \varepsilon^{- 1} f_{1, \varepsilon} u_{\varepsilon} - \varepsilon^{- 3 / 2} f_{2, \varepsilon} H_2 \left( \varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} u_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2 \right) . \end{eqnarray*}

We write \(u_{\varepsilon} = Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}\) with \(Y_{\varepsilon}\) as in (3), and perform a Taylor expansion of \(\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon} + \varepsilon^{1 / 2} v_{\varepsilon})\) around \(\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}\) up to the third order to get

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} u_{\varepsilon} & = & \eta_{\varepsilon} - \varepsilon^{- \frac{3}{2}} \tilde{F}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) - \varepsilon^{- 1} \tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) v_{\varepsilon} - \frac{1}{2} \varepsilon^{- \frac{1}{2}} \tilde{F}^{(2)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) v_{\varepsilon}^2 - \frac{1}{6} \tilde{F}^{(3)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) v_{\varepsilon}^3\\ & & - \varepsilon^{- 3 / 2} f_{0, \varepsilon} - \varepsilon^{- 1} f_{1, \varepsilon} (Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}) - \varepsilon^{- 1 / 2} f_{2, \varepsilon} (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} + 2 v_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}^2) - R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}) . \end{array} \) (11)

where \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon})\) is the remainder of the Taylor series and we used the fact that \(H_2 (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) = \varepsilon \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\). Notice that we stopped the Taylor expansion at the first term for which \(\varepsilon\) does not appear anymore with a negative exponent (that is \(\tilde{F}^{(3)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon})\)). One can then expect the remainder \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon})\) to converge to zero in some sense. On the other hand, all the other terms except \(\tilde{F}^{(3)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon})\) and \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon})\) appear to diverge in the limit \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\), but in analogy with well-known renormalization methods for random fields, we try to find a combination of them that can be made to converge in some function space. Define the following random fields:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lllllll} \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} Y_{\varepsilon} & := & - Y_{\varepsilon} + \eta_{\varepsilon} & \hspace{4em} & & & \\ \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} & := & \varepsilon^{- 1 / 2} f_{2, \varepsilon} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} & & \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} & := & \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}},\\ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} & := & \varepsilon^{- \frac{3}{2}} \tilde{F}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) & & \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} & := & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}},\\ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}} & := & \frac{1}{3} \varepsilon^{- 1} \tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) & & \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}} & := & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-9.pdf}}}\\ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-10.pdf}}} & := & \frac{1}{6} \varepsilon^{- \frac{1}{2}} \tilde{F}^{(2)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) & & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing} & := & \frac{1}{6} \tilde{F}^{(3)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon})\\ \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-11.pdf}}} & := & \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-12.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-13.pdf}}} - \bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-14.pdf}}} & & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-15.pdf}}} & := & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-16.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-17.pdf}}} - d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-18.pdf}}},\\ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-19.pdf}}} & := & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-20.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-21.pdf}}} - d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-22.pdf}}}, & & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-23.pdf}}} & := & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-24.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-25.pdf}}} {- d'_{\varepsilon}}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-26.pdf}}} Y_{\varepsilon} - d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-27.pdf}}}, \end{array} \) (12)

with \(Y_{\varepsilon}\) stationary solution, while \(Y_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}}, \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}}\) have \(0\) initial condition in \(t = 0\). The last four trees \(\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) are obtained from the others via the resonant Bony's paraproduct \(\circ\) recalled in Appendix A, and \(\bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), \(d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), \(d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), \({d'_{\varepsilon}}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), \(d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) are just \(\varepsilon\)-dependent constants whose exact value will matter only in Section 26. Indeed, in the scope of this section we only need the following relation to be verified:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rll} {d'_{\varepsilon}}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} & = & 2 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} + 3 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} . \end{array} \) (13)

The notation \(\)\(\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) denotes that this tree has finite chaos expansion and can be treated with the well-known techniques of [4] or [18] (we put a bar on \(\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) just because is the only tree obtained from \(\)\(\bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\))

With the definitions (12), equation (11) takes the form

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} v_{\varepsilon} & = & Y_{\varepsilon} - \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} - 3 Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} v_{\varepsilon} - 3 Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} v_{\varepsilon}^2 - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing} v_{\varepsilon}^3\\ & & - \varepsilon^{- 3 / 2} f_{0, \varepsilon} - \varepsilon^{- 1} f_{1, \varepsilon} (Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}) - \varepsilon^{- 1 / 2} f_{2, \varepsilon} (2 Y_{\varepsilon} v_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}^2) - R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}) . \end{array} \) (14)

At this point it is worth noting that the trivial case \(\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon} (x) = H_3 (x, \sigma^2_{\varepsilon})\) yields \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} = \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^3 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} = \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y^2_{\varepsilon} \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\), \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} = Y_{\varepsilon}\), \(Y^{\varnothing} = 1\). By comparing these random fields to the ones defined in [4] we can guess that \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}}, Y^{\varnothing}\) can be controlled respectively in \(\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{1 / 2 - \kappa}, \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 - \kappa}, \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}, \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \kappa}\) \(\forall \kappa > 0\) for any \(F_{\varepsilon}\) satisfying Assumption 1, and carry on the paracontrolled analysis of (14) as if it were the case. Clearly, the paracontrolled structure is robust and does not depend on how the terms \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}}, Y^{\varnothing}\) are defined as long as they have the desired regularity.

From these observations, we do not expect to be able to control the products \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} v_{\varepsilon}\), \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} v_{\varepsilon}^2\) and \(Y_{\varepsilon} v_{\varepsilon}\) in eq. (14) uniformly in \(\varepsilon > 0\). In order to proceed with the analysis we make the Ansatz:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} u_{\varepsilon} & = & Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon},\\ v_{\varepsilon} & = & - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} - \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} - 3 v_{\varepsilon} \prec\!\!\!\prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} + v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon} \end{array} \) (15)

and proceed to decompose the ill-defined products using the paracontrolled techniques recalled in Appendix A. We start by writing \(v_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} = v_{\varepsilon} \prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} + v_{\varepsilon} \succ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} + v_{\varepsilon} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}}\). The resonant term, together with Ansatz (15), yields:

\begin{eqnarray*} v_{\varepsilon} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} & = & - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} - \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} - 3 v_{\varepsilon} (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}})\\ & & - 3 \overline{\operatorname{com}}_1 (v_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-9.pdf}}}) + v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-10.pdf}}}, \end{eqnarray*}

with the definition and bounds of \(\overline{\operatorname{com}}_1 (\cdot, \cdot, \cdot)\) given in Lemma 26. Then we define

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} \hat{\diamond} v_{\varepsilon} & := & v_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} - v_{\varepsilon} \prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} + (3 v_{\varepsilon} d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} {+ d'_{\varepsilon}}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} Y_{\varepsilon} + d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} + \bar{d}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}})\\ & = & v_{\varepsilon} \succ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}} - \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-9.pdf}}} - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-10.pdf}}} - 3 v_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-11.pdf}}} + v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-12.pdf}}} - 3 \overline{\operatorname{com}}_1 (v_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-13.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-14.pdf}}}) . \end{array}\)

Moreover we have for \(v_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon}\):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{rll} v_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon} & = & \varphi_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon} - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} \prec Y_{\varepsilon} - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} \succ Y_{\varepsilon} - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}, \end{array}\)

where we introduced the shorthand \(\varphi_{\varepsilon} := v_{\varepsilon} + Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\). So we let

\(\displaystyle v_{\varepsilon} \diamond Y_{\varepsilon} := v_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon} + d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} = \varphi_{\varepsilon} Y_{\varepsilon} - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} \prec Y_{\varepsilon} - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} \succ Y_{\varepsilon} - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}},\)

Finally to analyse the product \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} v_{\varepsilon}^2\) we write

\(\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} v_{\varepsilon}^2 = Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}})^2 - 2 Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} \varphi_{\varepsilon} + Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} \varphi_{\varepsilon}^2,\)

and consider the products involving only \(Y^{\tau}\) factors: first

\(\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} = Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} \succ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} + Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} \prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} + Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}} + d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}} = : Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-9.pdf}}} \diamond Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-10.pdf}}} + d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-11.pdf}}},\)

and then we define the term \(\)\(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} \diamond (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}})^2\) as follows:

\begin{eqnarray*} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} \diamond (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}})^2 & := & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}})^2 - 2 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}}\\ & = & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}} \prec (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}})^2 + Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-9.pdf}}} \succ (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-10.pdf}}})^2 + Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-11.pdf}}} \circ (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-12.pdf}}} \circ Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-13.pdf}}}) + 2\operatorname{com}_1 (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-14.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-15.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-16.pdf}}}) + 2 Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-17.pdf}}} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-18.pdf}}}, \end{eqnarray*}

so that

\(\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} \diamond v_{\varepsilon}^2 := Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} v_{\varepsilon}^2 + 2 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} v_{\varepsilon} = Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} \diamond (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}})^2 - 2 (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} \diamond Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}}) \varphi_{\varepsilon} + Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}} \varphi_{\varepsilon}^2 .\)

We note also that

\(\displaystyle \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} v_{\varepsilon} = - \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} - \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} + \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon} - 3 v_{\varepsilon} \prec \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} - 3\operatorname{com}_3 (v_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}}) - 3\operatorname{com}_2 (v_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}}),\)

with \(\operatorname{com}_2 (\cdot, \cdot)\) and \(\operatorname{com}_3 (\cdot, \cdot)\) specified in Lemma 26 of the Appendix. Substituting these renormalized products into (14) and recalling the definition (9) for \(\lambda_{\varepsilon} = (\lambda_{0, \varepsilon}, \lambda_{1, \varepsilon}, \lambda_{2, \varepsilon}, \lambda_{3, \varepsilon})\), we obtain the following equation for \(v^{\natural}_{\varepsilon}\) :

\begin{eqnarray*} \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} v_{\varepsilon}^{\natural} & = & 3\operatorname{com}_3 (v_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}) + 3\operatorname{com}_2 (v_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}})\\ & & - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing} v_{\varepsilon}^3 - 3 Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} \diamond v_{\varepsilon}^2 - 3 Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} \hat{\diamond} v_{\varepsilon}\\ & & + Y_{\varepsilon} - \lambda_{2, \varepsilon} (2 v_{\varepsilon} \diamond Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}^2)\\ & & - \lambda_{1, \varepsilon} (Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}) + [9 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} + 6 d_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} {- 3 d'_{\varepsilon}}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}}] v_{\varepsilon} - \lambda_{0, \varepsilon} - R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}), \end{eqnarray*}

where we can use the constraint (13) to remove the term proportional to \(v_{\varepsilon}\). Summarizing, we obtain the following equation, together with Ansatz (15):

\(\displaystyle \left\{ \begin{array}{lll} v_{\varepsilon} & = & - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} - \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} - 3 v_{\varepsilon} \prec\!\!\!\prec Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} + v^{\natural}\\ \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} v_{\varepsilon}^{\natural} & = & U (\lambda_{\varepsilon}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon} ; v_{\varepsilon}, v_{\varepsilon}^{\natural}) - R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}) \end{array} \right. \) (16)

with initial condition \(v_{\varepsilon, 0} = u_{0, \varepsilon} - Y_{\varepsilon} (0)\) and \(U\) given by

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} U (\lambda_{\varepsilon}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon} ; v_{\varepsilon}, v_{\varepsilon}^{\natural}) & : = & 3\operatorname{com}_3 (v_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}) + 3\operatorname{com}_2 (v_{\varepsilon}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}}) - Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing} v_{\varepsilon}^3\\ & & - 3 Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} \diamond v_{\varepsilon}^2 - 3 Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} \hat{\diamond} v_{\varepsilon} + Y_{\varepsilon} - \lambda_{2, \varepsilon} (2 v_{\varepsilon} \diamond Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}^2)\\ & & - \lambda_{1, \varepsilon} (Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}) - \lambda_{0, \varepsilon} - R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}) . \end{array} \) (17)

The enhanced noise vector \(\mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}\) is defined by

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}\) \(\displaystyle :=\) \(\displaystyle (Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}}, \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}}, \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}})\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle \)
\(\displaystyle \mathcal{X}_T\) \(\displaystyle :=\) \(\displaystyle C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \kappa} \times C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \frac{1}{2} - \kappa} \times \left( C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 - \kappa} \right)^2 \times \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em}_T^{1 / 2 - \kappa} \times \left( C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \kappa} \right)^3 \times C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \frac{1}{2} - \kappa}\) (18)

for every \(\kappa > 0\), \(T > 0\). We use the notation \(\| \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon} \|_{\mathcal{X}_T} = \sum_{\tau} \| \mathbb{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon} \|_{\mathcal{X}^{\tau}}\) for the associated norm where \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}\) is a generic tree in \(\mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}\). The homogeneities \(| \tau | \in \mathbb{R}\) are given by

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|} \hline Y_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} & = & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing} & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} & \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} & \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}} & Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}}\\ \hline | \tau | & = & 0 & - 1 / 2 & - 1 & - 1 & 1 / 2 & 0 & 0 & 0 & - 1 / 2\\ \hline \end{array}\)

Note that for every \(\varepsilon > 0\) eq. (16) is equivalent to eq. (5).

Remark 6. The paracontrolled structure we developed in this section is the same as in the work of Catellier and Chouk [4], plus an additive source term (which is \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon})\) in equation (16)). Therefore, there exists \(T = T \left( \| \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon} \|_{\mathcal{X}_T}, \| u_{\varepsilon, 0} \|_{\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}}, | \lambda_{\varepsilon} | \right)\) such that we can define for \(\alpha \in (1 / 2, 2 / 3)\), \(p \in [4, \infty)\)\(\), \(\gamma > \frac{1}{4} + \frac{3}{2} \kappa\) a solution map

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{llll} \Gamma : & \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa} \times \mathcal{X}^{\tau} \times \mathbb{R}^4 \times \mathcal{M}^{\gamma, p}_T L^p (\mathbb{T}^3) & \rightarrow & C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} (\mathbb{T}^3)\\ & (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, \lambda_{\varepsilon}, \mathcal{R}) & \mapsto & u_{\varepsilon} \end{array}\)

so that \(u_{\varepsilon} = \Gamma (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, \lambda_{\varepsilon}, \mathcal{R})\) with \(u_{\varepsilon} = Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}\) and \(v_{\varepsilon}\) that solves (16) with the remainder \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon})\) replaced by \(\mathcal{R}\). The space \(\mathcal{M}^{\gamma}_T L^p (\mathbb{T}^3)\) is specified in Appendix A.1. Indeed, we can use Lemma 20 and Lemma 22 to control \(I\mathcal{R}\) as

\(\displaystyle \|I\mathcal{R}\|_{\mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em}^{1 / 2, 1 + 2 \kappa}_T} \lesssim T^{\delta} \| \mathcal{R} \|_{\mathcal{M}^{\gamma, p}_T L^p}\)

for \(\delta > 0\) small enough, and thanks to this bound it is easy to see that the the fixed point procedure of Section 3 of [4] still holds with a fixed additive source term \(\mathcal{R}\). In the same way, the continuity of the solution map \(\Gamma\) follows easily as in Theorem 1.2 of [4].

2.2Identification of the limit

In order to identify interesting limits for equation (5), we introduce \(\forall \lambda = (\lambda_0, \lambda_1, \lambda_2, \lambda_3) \in \mathbb{R}^4\) the enhanced noise \(\mathbb{Y} (\lambda)\) which is constructed from universal noises \(X^{\tau}\) as

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} \mathbb{Y} (\lambda) & := & (Y^{\varnothing} (\lambda), Y^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (\lambda), Y^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} (\lambda), \bar{Y}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} (\lambda), Y^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} (\lambda), Y^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} (\lambda), Y^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} (\lambda), Y^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}} (\lambda), Y^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}} (\lambda))\\ & := & (\lambda_3, \lambda_3 X, \lambda_3 X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-9.pdf}}}, \lambda_2 X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-10.pdf}}}, \lambda_3 X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-11.pdf}}}, (\lambda_3)^2 X^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-12.pdf}}}, (\lambda_3)^2 X^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-13.pdf}}}, \lambda_3 \lambda_2 X^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-14.pdf}}}, (\lambda_3)^2 X^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-15.pdf}}}) \end{array} \) (19)

where \(X\) is the stationary solution to to the linear equation \(\mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} X = - X + \xi\) and \(\xi\) is the time-space white noise on \(\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3\). We will sometimes use the shorter notation \(\mathbb{Y} (\lambda) = (Y^{\tau} (\lambda))_{\tau}\) for (19).

We define the universal fields \(X^{\tau}\) through their Littlewood-Paley decomposition \(\forall (t, \bar{x}) \in \mathbb{R}^+ \times \mathbb{T}^3\) as:

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} & := & \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^3 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}, \qquad \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} = X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} \quad \text{with $X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} (t = 0) = 0$},\\ X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} & := & \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}},\\ \Delta_q X^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) & := & \Delta_q (1 - J_0) (X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-7.pdf}}} \circ X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-8.pdf}}}) (t, \bar{x}) = \int_{\zeta_1, \zeta_2} (1 - J_0) (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^2 (\zeta_1) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^2 (\zeta_2) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \mu_{q, \zeta_1, \zeta_2},\\ \Delta_q X^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-9.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) & := & \Delta_q (X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-10.pdf}}} \circ X) (t, \bar{x}) = \int_{\zeta_1, \zeta_2} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^3 (\zeta_1) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} X (\zeta_2) \mu_{q, \zeta_1, \zeta_2},\\ \Delta_q X^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-11.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) & := & \int_{\zeta_1, \zeta_2} (1 - J_1) (\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^3 (\zeta_1) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^2 (\zeta_2) \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}) \mu_{q, \zeta_1, \zeta_2} \hspace{1em}\\ & & + 6 \int_{s , x} [\Delta_q X (t + s, \bar{x} - x) - \Delta_q X (t, \bar{x})] P_s (x) [C (s, x)]^2, \end{array} \) (20)

where as before \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \cdot \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) stands for the Wick product, \(\zeta_i = (x_i, s_i) \in \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3\), \(C (\cdot, \cdot)\) is the covariance of \(X\) and \(\mu_{q, \zeta_1, \zeta_2}\) is the measure

\(\displaystyle \mu_{q, \zeta_1, \zeta_2} := \delta (t - s_2) \mathrm{d} \zeta_1 \mathrm{d} \zeta_2 \int_{x, y} K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) \sum_{i \sim j} K_{i, x} (y) K_{j, x} (x_2) P_{t - s_1} (y - x_1)\)

with the usual heat kernel \(P_t (x) = \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{3 / 2}} e^{- \frac{| x |^2}{4 t}} \mathbb{1}_{t \geqslant 0}\). We commit an abuse of notation by writing \(X (\zeta)\) since \(X\) is actually a distribution in space: the integrals in (20) should obviously be intended as functionals.

Standard computations (see e.g. [4] or [18]) show that, \(\forall \lambda \in \mathbb{R}^4\) and for any \(T > 0\), \(0 < \kappa < \kappa'\)

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{Y} (\lambda) \in C^{\kappa}_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \frac{1}{2} - 2 \kappa'} \times \left( C^{\kappa}_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 - 2 \kappa'} \right)^2 \times C^{\kappa}_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{\frac{1}{2} - 2 \kappa'} \times \left( C^{\kappa}_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{0 - 2 \kappa'} \right)^2 \times C^{\kappa}_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \frac{1}{2} - 2 \kappa'}_T,\)

almost surely.

Using the paracontrolled structure we developed in Section 2.1 we can identify the limiting solution \(u (\lambda)\) introduced in Theorem 2.

Theorem 7. The family of random fields \(u_{\varepsilon}\) given by the solutions of eq. (5) converges in law and locally in time to a limiting random field \(u (\lambda)\) in the space \(C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} (\mathbb{T}^3)\) for every \(1 / 2 < \alpha < 2 / 3\). The limiting random field \(u (\lambda)\) solves the paracontrolled equation

\(\displaystyle \left\{ \begin{array}{lll} u (\lambda) & = & X + v (\lambda)\\ v (\lambda) & = & - \lambda_3 X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} - \lambda_2 X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} - 3 \lambda_3 v (\lambda) \prec\!\!\!\prec X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}} + v^{\natural} (\lambda)\\ \mathscr{L} \hspace{.2em} v^{\natural} (\lambda) & = & U (\lambda, \mathbb{Y} (\lambda) ; v (\lambda), v^{\natural} (\lambda))\\ v^{\natural} (\lambda) (t = 0) & = & v_0 + \lambda_3 X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}} (t = 0) + \lambda_2 X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-5.pdf}}} (t = 0) + 3 \lambda_3 v_{\varepsilon, 0} \prec X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-6.pdf}}} (t = 0) \end{array} \right. \) (21)

with \(U\) defined in (17) and \(v_0 = u_0 - X (t = 0)\).

Proof. Fix \(T > 0\). Let \(u_{\varepsilon} = Y_{\varepsilon} + v_{\varepsilon}\) be the solution of eq. (5) for fixed \(\varepsilon > 0\), which is seen to be unique in the (\(\varepsilon\)-dependent) time interval \([0, T_{\varepsilon}]\) by a usual fixed-point argument on the original equation (without resorting to the paracontrolled decomposition). Let \(u_{\varepsilon} = \Gamma (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, \lambda_{\varepsilon}, R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}))\) on \([0, T_{\varepsilon}]\) with \(\Gamma\) defined as in Remark 6 and \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon})\) seen as an exogenous source term. We know from the a-priori estimations of Section 4.2 that there exists a time \(T_{\star} = T_{\star} \left( \| \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon} \|_{\mathcal{X}_T}, \| u_{\varepsilon, 0} \|_{\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}}, | \lambda_{\varepsilon} | \right)\) and a family of events \((\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon > 0}\) such that \(\mathbb{P} (\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}) \rightarrow 1\) for \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\) and we can control \(\| v_{\varepsilon} \|_{\mathcal{M}^{1 / 4 + 3 \kappa / 2}_{T_{\star}} L^{\infty}}\). Thus, we can control the \(L^{\infty}\) norm of \(v_{\varepsilon} (t)\) in \([T_{\varepsilon} / 2, T_{\varepsilon}]\) and extend the solution \(v_{\varepsilon}\) on \([0, T_{\star}]\) for every \(\varepsilon\). Denote by \(u_{\varepsilon}^{\star} = \Gamma_{\star} (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, \lambda_{\varepsilon}, R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}))\) the process \(u_{\varepsilon}\) stopped at time \(T_{\star}\) and \(\Gamma_{\star}\) the corresponding stopped solution map.

Note that \(u (\lambda)\) solves the same equation as \(u_{\varepsilon}^{\star}\)\(\) with \(\mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}\) replaced by \(\mathbb{Y} (\lambda)\)\(\), \(u_{\varepsilon, 0}\) replaced by \(u_0\), \(\lambda_{\varepsilon}\) replaced by \(\lambda\) and \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}) = 0\). So \(u (\lambda) = u^{\star} = \Gamma_{\star} (u_0, \mathbb{Y} (\lambda), \lambda, 0)\) up to time \(T_{\star}\). Let us introduce the random field \(\bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star} = \Gamma_{\star} (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, \lambda_{\varepsilon}, 0)\) with \(\bar{v}^{\star}_{\varepsilon} = \bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star} - Y_{\varepsilon}\) that solves the paracontrolled equation (16) but with remainder \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}) = 0\).

Consider the \(n\)-tuple of random variables \((u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, u_{\varepsilon}^{\star}, \bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star})\) and let \(\mu_{\varepsilon}\) be its law on \(\mathcal{Z}= \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} \times \mathcal{X}_T \times \left( C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} \right)^2\) conditionally on \(\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}\). Observe that \(\Gamma_{\star}\) is continuous as discussed in Remark 6, and this gives that \(\forall \delta > 0\), \(\mu_{\varepsilon} \left( \| u_{\varepsilon}^{\star} - \bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star} \|_{C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha}} > \delta \right) \rightarrow 0\) as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\). Indeed \(R_{\varepsilon} (v_{\varepsilon}) \rightarrow 0\) in probability in the space \(\mathcal{M}^{\gamma, p}_{T_{\star}} L^p (\mathbb{T}^3)\) by Lemma 19. This shows that \(\mu_{\varepsilon}\) concentrates on \(\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} \times \mathcal{X}_T \times \left\{ (z, z) \in \left( C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} \right)^2 \right\}\). Let \(\mu\) any accumulation point of \((\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\). Then \(\mu \left( \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} \times \mathcal{X}_T \times \left\{ (z, z) \in \left( C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} \right)^2 \right\} \right) = 1\). The apriori estimations of Section 4.2 yield the tightness of \(\mu_{\varepsilon}\) and from the concentration of \(\mu\) on the diagonal we know that there exists a subsequence such that for any test function \(\varphi\),

\(\displaystyle \int_{\mathcal{Z}} \varphi (x, y, z, t) \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon} (x, y, z, t) \rightarrow \int_{\mathcal{Z}} \varphi (x, y, z, t) \mathrm{d} \mu (x, y, z, t) = \int_{\mathcal{Z}} \varphi (x, y, t, t) \mathrm{d} \mu (x, y, z, t) . \) (22)

Moreover, still along subsequences we have that for any bounded continuous function \(\varphi\)

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} (\varphi (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, \bar{u}_{\varepsilon}^{\star})) =\mathbb{E} (\varphi (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, \Gamma_{\star} (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}, \lambda_{\varepsilon}, 0))) \rightarrow \mathbb{E} (\varphi (u_0, \mathbb{Y} (\lambda), \Gamma_{\star} (u_0, \mathbb{Y} (\lambda), \lambda, 0)))\)

since by Theorem 8 the vector \(\mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}\) converges in law to \(\mathbb{Y} (\lambda)\), \(u_{\varepsilon, 0}\) to \(u_0\), and \(\Gamma_{\star}\) is a continuous function as discussed in Remark 6. This shows that

\(\displaystyle \int_{\mathcal{Z}} \varphi (x, y, t, t) \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon} (x, y, z, t) \rightarrow \int_{\mathcal{Z}} \varphi (x, y, \Gamma_{\star} (x, y, \lambda, 0), \Gamma_{\star} (x, y, \lambda, 0)) \mathrm{d} \mu (x, y, z, t) \) (23)

and then by comparing (22) and (23) we can conclude that there exists a subsequence such that

\(\displaystyle \int_{\mathcal{Z}} \varphi (x, y, z, t) \mathrm{d} \mu_{\varepsilon} (x, y, z, t) \rightarrow \int_{\mathcal{Z}} \varphi (x, y, \Gamma_{\star} (x, y, \lambda, 0), \Gamma_{\star} (x, y, \lambda, 0)) \mathrm{d} \mu (x, y, z, t) .\)

We can identify the limit distribution \(\mu\) by noting that since \(\mathbb{P} (\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}) \rightarrow 1\) we have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} [\psi (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}) |\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}] = \frac{\mathbb{E} [\psi (u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}) \mathbb{I}_{\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}}]}{\mathbb{P} (\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon})} \rightarrow \mathbb{E} [\psi (u_0, \mathbb{Y} (\lambda))]\)

for any test function \(\psi\). So the first two marginals of \(\mu\) have the law of \((u_0, \mathbb{Y} (\lambda))\) and they are independent since \((u_{\varepsilon, 0}, \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon})\) are independent for any \(\varepsilon\). Calling \(\nu\) the law of \((u_0, \mathbb{Y} (\lambda))\) we have that

\(\displaystyle \int_{\mathcal{Z}} \varphi (x, y, z, t) \mathrm{d} \mu (x, y, z, t) = \int_{\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha} \times \mathcal{X}_T} \varphi (x, y, \Gamma_{\star} (x, y, \lambda, 0), \Gamma_{\star} (x, y, \lambda, 0)) \mathrm{d} \nu (x, y)\)

which implies that \(\mu\) is unique and that the whole family \((\mu_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon}\) converges to \(\mu\). We can conclude that \(u^{\star}_{\varepsilon} \rightarrow u^{\star}\) in law with \(u^{\star} = u (\lambda)\) up to the time \(T_{\star} \left( \| \mathbb{Y} (\lambda) \|_{\mathcal{X}_T}, \| u_0 \|_{\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}}, | \lambda | \right)\) since the function \(T^{\star}\) is lower semicontinuous (as obtained from the a-priori estimates).\(\Box\)

3Convergence of the enhanced noise

This is the central section of the paper, in which we present a new method to estimate certain random fields that do not have a finite chaos decomposition, and we apply it to the treatment of the random fields \(\mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}\) of (12).

3.1An example of convergence

We choose to give first a complete example (the convergence of the tree \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) to \(\)\(Y^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (\lambda)\)) in order to put in evidence the main idea in the proof of Theorem 8. Recall its definition (12):

\(\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} = \frac{\varepsilon^{- 1}}{3} \tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}),\)

with \(\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}\) being the first derivative of the centered function \(\tilde{F}_{\varepsilon}\) defined in (6). Since \(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x} H_n (x, \sigma^2_{\varepsilon}) = n\)\(H_{n - 1} (x, \sigma^2_{\varepsilon})\) the Wiener chaos decomposition of \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) reads:

\(\displaystyle \frac{\varepsilon^{- 1}}{3} \tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon})\) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \frac{\varepsilon^{- 1}}{3} \sum_{n \geqslant 3} nf_{n, \varepsilon} H_{n - 1} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2)\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle \varepsilon^{- 1} f_{3, \varepsilon} H_2 (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) + \frac{\varepsilon^{- 1}}{3} \sum_{n \geqslant 4} nf_{n, \varepsilon} H_{n - 1} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2)\)
\(\displaystyle \) \(\displaystyle =\) \(\displaystyle f_{3, \varepsilon} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} + \frac{\varepsilon^{- 1}}{3} \sum_{n \geqslant 4} nf_{n, \varepsilon} H_{n - 1} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2),\) (24)

where \(\mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} \cdot \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) is the Wiener product and we used the fact that \(\varepsilon^{- \frac{n}{2}} H_n (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) = \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^n \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\). Now one can use hypercontractivity (as done in [10],[11]) to control the \(L^p\) norm of each chaos order by its \(L^2\) norms. However this strategy does not give useful bounds for the infinite series in the second term of (24). Instead, we just observe that

\(\displaystyle \sum_{n \geqslant 4} nf_{n, \varepsilon} H_{n - 1} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon}, \sigma_{\varepsilon}^2) = (\operatorname{id}- J_0 - \ldots - J_2) \tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}),\)

where \(J_i\) is the projection of on the \(i\)-th chaos, and look for a different way to write this remainder. One of the main insights of this paper \(\)is that we can write it as:

\(\displaystyle (\operatorname{id}- J_0 - \ldots - J_2) \tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) = \delta^3 G_{[1]}^{[3]} \mathrm{D}^3 \tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon})\)

where \(\mathrm{D}\), \(\delta\) are the Malliavin derivative and divergence operators, and \(G_{[1]}^{[3]} = (1 - L)^{- 1} (2 - L)^{- 1} (3 - L)^{- 1}\) with \(L\) the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator. This is proven in Lemma 37.

To compute the Malliavin derivative of \(\tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon})\) we observe that for every \(\varepsilon > 0\) \((t, x) \in \mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3\) there exists \(h_{(t, x)} \in L^2 (\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3)\) such that the Gaussian random variable \(Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)} := Y_{\varepsilon} (t, x)\)\(\) can be written as

\(\displaystyle Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)} \overset{\text{law}}{=} \langle \xi, h_{(t, x)} \rangle . \) (25)

Here \(\xi\) is the Gaussian white noise on \(\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3\), which can be seen as a Gaussian Hilbert space \(\langle \xi, h \rangle_{h \in H} = \{ W (h) \}_{h \in H}\) indexed by the Hilbert space \(H := L^2 (\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{T}^3)\). This is the framework in which we apply the Malliavin calculus results of Appendix 26. Notice that by construction

\(\displaystyle \langle h_{(t, x)}, h_{(t', x')} \rangle = C_{\varepsilon} (t - t', x - x') := \mathbb{E} [Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)} Y_{\varepsilon, (t', x')}] . \) (26)

The function \(h_{(t, x)}\) can actually be written as the space-time convolution

\(\displaystyle h_{(t, x)} = \check{P} \ast \psi_{\varepsilon} (t, x),\)

with \(\check{P} (t, x) = \frac{1}{(4 \pi t)^{3 / 2}} e^{- \frac{| x |^2}{4 t}} e^{- t} \mathbb{1}_{t \geqslant 0}\) and \(\psi_{\varepsilon}\) such that \(\eta_{\varepsilon} = \psi_{\varepsilon} \ast \xi\), \(\psi_{\varepsilon} (t, x) = \varepsilon^{- 5 / 2} \psi (\varepsilon^{- 2} t, \varepsilon^{- 1} x)\). We omit the dependence on \(\varepsilon\) of \(h_{(t, x)}\) not to burden the notation.

Going back to the calculations, we obtain from (25) that \(\mathrm{D} \tilde{F}^{(1)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) = \varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} \tilde{F}^{(2)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) h .\) \(\)Then (noting that \(\tilde{F}^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} = F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}\)):

\(\displaystyle \begin{array}{lll} Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} & = & f_{3, \varepsilon} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} + \frac{\varepsilon^{1 / 2}}{3} \delta^3 G_{[1]}^{[3]} F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon}) h^{\otimes 3}\\ & = & f_{3, \varepsilon} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}} + \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}}, \end{array} . \) (27)

It can be easily seen from (25) that \(Y_{\varepsilon}\) has the same law of a time-space mollification of \(X\) by convolution (with \(X\) defined in Section 2.2). Then the convergence in law of \(f_{3, \varepsilon} \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} Y_{\varepsilon}^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) to \(Y^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (\lambda)\) can be easily established by standard techniques (see [4] or [18]). We are only left to show that \(\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) in (27) converges to zero in \(C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 - \kappa}\).\(\)

It is well known (see Section 3.2 for details) that in order to control the norm of \(\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (t, \cdot)\) for \(t \in (0, T]\) in the Besov space \(\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- \alpha - \kappa}\) \(\forall \kappa > 0\) and in probability, it is enough to have suitable estimates for

\(\displaystyle \sup_{x \in \mathbb{T}^3} \left\| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)} = \left\| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)},\)

for any \(\bar{x} \in \mathbb{T}^3\) since \(\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) is stationary in space. We then proceed to compute:

\begin{eqnarray*} \left\| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)} & = & \frac{\varepsilon^{1 / 2}}{3} \left\| \delta^3 G_{[1]}^{[3]} \int K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) h_{(t, x)}^{\otimes 3} \mathrm{d} x \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)} \end{eqnarray*}

where \(K_{q, \bar{x}} (x)\) is the kernel associated to the Littlewood-Paley block \(\Delta_q\). Observe that \(\| F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) \|^p_{L^p} = \int_{\mathbb{R}} | F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (z) |^p \gamma (\mathrm{d} z)\) where \(\gamma (\mathrm{d} z)\) is the density of a centered Gaussian with variance \(\sigma^2_{\varepsilon}\). This norm is then finite by the bound (8) of Assumption 1.

Another fundamental idea of this work is that we can “estimate out” the bounded term \(\| F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{1 / 2} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) \|_{L^p}\), which has an infinite chaos decomposition, and obtain a standard \(\Phi^4_3\) diagram that can be treated with well understood techniques. Using Lemma 38 and Corollary 42, one has

\begin{eqnarray*} & & \left\| \delta^3 G_{[1]}^{[3]} \int K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) h_{(t, x)}^{\otimes 3} \mathrm{d} x \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)}\\ & \lesssim & \sum_{k = 0}^3 \left\| \mathrm{D}^k G_{[1]}^{[3]} \int K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) h_{(t, x)}^{\otimes 3} \mathrm{d} x \right\|_{L^p (\Omega, H^{\otimes 3 + k})}\\ & \lesssim & \left\| \int K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) h_{(t, x)}^{\otimes 3} \mathrm{d} x \right\|_{L^p (\Omega, H^{\otimes 3})} . \end{eqnarray*}

Then note that we can decompose the norm \(\| u \|_{L^p (\Omega, H^{\otimes 3})} = \| \| u \|^2_{H^{\otimes 3}} \|^{1 / 2}_{L^{p / 2}}\) for \(u \in L^p (\Omega, H^{\otimes 3})\) and since the norm of the Hilbert space \(H^{\otimes 3}\) is given by the scalar product \(\| h^{\otimes 3} \|^2_{H^{\otimes 3}} = \langle h, h \rangle_H^3\) we obtain

\begin{eqnarray*} \left\| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) \right\|_{L^p} & \lesssim & \varepsilon^{1 / 2} \left\| \left\| \int K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) h_{(t, x)}^{\otimes 3} \mathrm{d} x \right\|_{H^{\otimes 3}}^2 \right\|_{L^{p / 2}}^{1 / 2} \end{eqnarray*}
\(\displaystyle \lesssim \left[ \varepsilon \int | K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) K_{q, \bar{x}} (x') | \left\| F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x')}) \right\|_{L^{p / 2}} | \langle h_{(t, x)}, h_{(t, x')} \rangle |^3 \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} x' \right]^{\frac{1}{2}} .\)

The norm containing \(\tilde{F}^{(4)}_{\varepsilon}\) can then be easily estimated using Hölder's inequality as

\(\displaystyle \left\| F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x')}) \right\|_{L^{p / 2}} \leqslant \left\| F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x)}) \right\|_{L^p} \left\| F^{(4)}_{\varepsilon} (\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}} Y_{\varepsilon, (t, x')}) \right\|_{L^p} \lesssim 1.\)

This yields

\(\displaystyle \left\| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)} \lesssim \left[ \varepsilon \int | K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) K_{q, \bar{x}} (x') | | \langle h_{(t, x)}, h_{(t, x')} \rangle |^3 \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} x' \right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\)

which is a standard \(\Phi^4_3\) diagram that can be analysed with the techniques of [8] (recalled in Appendix A.3). We just remark that \(\langle h_{(t, x)}, h_{(t, x')} \rangle = C_{\varepsilon} (0, x - x')\) and the bound \(\varepsilon | C_{\varepsilon} (t, x) | \lesssim 1\) of Lemma 31 yields \(\forall \delta \in (0, 1)\):

\begin{eqnarray*} \left\| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (t, \bar{x}) \right\|_{L^p (\Omega)} & \lesssim & \varepsilon^{\frac{\delta}{2}} \left[ \int | K_{q, \bar{x}} (x) K_{q, \bar{x}} (x') | | C_{\varepsilon} (0, x - x') |^{2 + \delta} \mathrm{d} x \mathrm{d} x' \right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\\ & \lesssim & \varepsilon^{\frac{\delta}{2}} 2^{(1 + \delta / 2) q} \end{eqnarray*}

and then \(\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (t, \cdot)\) converges to zero in probability in the space \(\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 - \delta / 2}\) \(\forall \delta \in (0, 1)\), as \(\varepsilon \rightarrow 0\). The time regularity of \(\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\) needed to obtain the convergence in \(C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 - \delta / 2}\) does not need new ideas, and it is done in Section 3.3.

The method shown in this section is valid verbatim for the trees \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\varnothing}\), \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{0.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}\), while for the composite trees in (12) (namely \(Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}}, \bar{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-3.pdf}}}, Y_{\varepsilon}^{\resizebox{1.5em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-4.pdf}}}\)) that are obtained via paraproducts of simple trees, one has to be able to write the remainder \(\hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau}\) as an iterated Skorohod integral \(\delta^n (\ldots)\) in order to exploit the boundedness of this operator. Moreover, these trees require a second renormalization (on top of the Wick ordering) which is not easy to control for infinite chaos decompositions. We deal with both these difficulties introducing the product formula (85), which allows to write products of iterated Skorohod integrals as combinations of iterated Skorohod integrals. The details and calculations for composite trees can be found in Section 3.4.

3.2Main theorem and overview of the proof

Theorem 8. Under Assumption 1 there exists \(C > 0\) such that for any \(p \in [2, \infty)\) we have \(\| \mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon} \|_{\mathcal{X}_T} < C\) in \(L^p (\Omega)\). Moreover, \(\mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon} \rightarrow \mathbb{Y} (\lambda) \in \mathcal{X}_T\) and \(Y_{\varepsilon} \rightarrow X \in C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}\) in law.

The rest of Section 26 is dedicated to the proof of Theorem 8.

From the definition \(\mathbb{Y} (\lambda) = (Y^{\tau} (\lambda))_{\tau}\) of (19) it is clear that we need to prove that \(Y^{\tau}_{\varepsilon} \rightarrow Y^{\tau} (\lambda)\) for every tree \(\tau\). Note that we can write each tree \(Y^{\tau} (\lambda)\) as \(Y^{\tau} (\lambda) = f_{\tau} (\lambda) K^{\tau} (X)\) for a the measurable function \(K^{\tau}\) of the Gaussian process \(X \in C_T \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{- 1 / 2 - \kappa}\) defined (20), and a suitable deterministic function \(f_{\tau} (\lambda)\) of \(\lambda\). For example, we can write \(Y^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (\lambda) = \lambda_3 \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) with \(K^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (X) = X^{\resizebox{1em}{!}{\includegraphics{image-2.pdf}}} = \mathopen{\unicode{x27E6}} X^2 \mathclose{\unicode{x27E7}}\) and \(f_{\resizebox{6pt}{4.5pt}{\includegraphics{image-1.pdf}}} (\lambda) = \lambda_3\).

We will show (eqs. (36) and (44)) that every random field \(Y^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}\) defined in (12) can be decomposed with the same functions \(f_{\tau} (\cdot)\) and \(K^{\tau}\) as

\(\displaystyle Y^{\tau}_{\varepsilon} = f_{\tau} (\lambda_{\varepsilon}) K^{\tau} (Y_{\varepsilon}) + \hat{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon} \) (28)

where \(\hat{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}\) are suitable remainder terms. For all \(p \geqslant 2\) it is well-known (see [4],[8]) that the term \(f_{\tau} (\lambda_{\varepsilon}) K^{\tau} (Y_{\varepsilon})\) is uniformly bounded in \(L^p (\Omega ; \mathcal{X}^{\tau})\) (with \(\mathcal{X}^{\tau}\) given by (18)). Thus, we will prove that \(\hat{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon}\) converges to zero in \(L^p (\Omega ; \mathcal{X}^{\tau})\). This can be done by showing that, by Besov embedding, for \(p \in [2, \infty)\) and \(\forall \alpha < | \tau |\) we have

\(\displaystyle \mathbb{E} (\| \hat{Y}^{\tau}_{\varepsilon} (t) \|_{\mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{\alpha - 3 / p}}^p) \lesssim \mathbb{E} (\| \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} (t) \|^p_{B^{\alpha}_{p, p}}) \leqslant \sum_q 2^{\alpha pq} \int_{\mathbb{T}^3} \| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} (t, x) \|^p_{L^p (\Omega)} \mathrm{d} x \rightarrow 0 \) (29)

thanks to the stationarity of the process \(Y (t, x)\). In order to prove the bound (29) it suffices to show that \(\forall t \in [0, T]\)

\(\displaystyle \sum_q 2^{\alpha pq} \sup_x \| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} (t, x) \|^p_{L^p (\Omega)} \rightarrow 0 \quad \text{as $\varepsilon \rightarrow 0$,} \) (30)

which is one of the key estimation of this paper and will be performed in Sections 3.3 and 3.4.2.

In order to obtain uniform convergence for \(t \in [0, T]\) it suffices to show that \(\forall \sigma \in [0, 1 / 2]\), \(q \geqslant - 1\):

\(\displaystyle \sup_x \| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} (t, x) - \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} (s, x) \|^p_{L^p (\Omega)} \leqslant C_{\varepsilon} | t - s |^{\sigma p} 2^{- (\alpha - 2 \sigma) pq} \quad \text{with $C_{\varepsilon} \rightarrow 0$} . \) (31)

Indeed, by the Garsia-Rodemich-Rumsey inequality we obtain for \(\delta > 0\) small enough and \(p\) large enough

\begin{eqnarray*} \sup_{\varepsilon} \mathbb{E} (\| \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} \|^p_{C_T^{\sigma - 2 / p} B^{\alpha - 2 \sigma - \delta}_{p, p}}) & \leqslant & T^2 \sum_q 2^{(\alpha - 2 \sigma - \delta) pq} \sup_{s < t \in [0, T]} \sup_x \frac{\| \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} (t, x) - \Delta_q \hat{Y}_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} (s, x) \|_{L^p (\Omega)}^p}{| t - s |^{\sigma p}}\\ & \leqslant & C_{\varepsilon} T^2 \sum_q 2^{- \delta pq} \end{eqnarray*}

which by Besov embedding yields an estimation on \(\mathbb{E} \left( \| Y_{\varepsilon}^{\tau} \|_{C_T^{\sigma - \kappa / 2} \mathscr{C} \hspace{.1em}^{\alpha - 2 \sigma - \kappa}} \right)\) for \(\kappa > 0\) small enough. This gives us the necessary tightness to claim that \(\mathbb{Y}_{\varepsilon}\) has weak limits along subsequences.

The only thing left after proving (28), (30) and (31) is that for each \(\tau\) we have \(K^{\tau} (Y_{\varepsilon}) \rightarrow K^{\tau} (X)\) in law. However this is clear and already well-known, since by hypothesis we can introduce a space-time convolution regularisation of \(X\) (let's call it \(X_{\varepsilon}\)) which has the same law of \(Y_{\varepsilon}\) for any \(\varepsilon > 0\). This yields immediately the convergence \(Y_{\varepsilon} \rightarrow X\) in law. At this point an approximation argument gives that \(K^{\tau} (Y_{\varepsilon})\) has the same law of \(K^{\tau} (X_{\varepsilon})\).
